As a wider point, beyond simply expecting tighter credit spreads in the hard currency 
sovereign space, further upward momentum in the ratings space should still be 
supportive for widening the investor base for the CCA region, allowing more international 
issuance in the primary market at the sovereign, quasi-sovereign and corporate level. It 
should also encourage more investment on a broader scale (beyond hot-money and 
speculative portfolio flows), while continuing to increase investor interest in local 
currency markets. Kazakhstan has been placed on Index Watch-Positive for potential 
inclusion in JP Morgan’s GBI-EM local currency debt index and is currently part of the 
new frontier local-currency government bond index (GBI-EM Edge), while Uzbekistan has 
seen its soum-denominated sovereign international bonds included in the GBI-EM.

### Page 9

CCA macro and credit November 2026

9 
CCA macro: Resilient, but 
increasingly divergent 
The region’s improving sovereign ratings reflects genuine progress. But, as the preceding 
ratings discussion indicates, investors have already priced in much of this improvement. 
In our view, the key macro questions are what is underpinning the resilience of individual 
economies, how durable those supports are and where their trajectories are starting to 
diverge. 
Our 2027 macro outlook remains constructive on regional growth and currencies. This 
fits into ING’s global view of another potentially resilient year, despite continued power 
struggles around politics, energy, technology and trade. The CCA region has so far 
navigated this difficult global setting better than might have been expected, benefiting 
from strong domestic demand, commodity revenues, remittances, redirected trade and 
capital inflows. Positive real rates and generally strong sovereign buffers should continue 
to limit the transmission of higher core rates into regional currencies and monetary 
policy. Kazakhstan and Uzbekistan retain room for cautious rate cuts, Azerbaijan should 
remain broadly on hold, while Armenia and Kyrgyzstan face greater tightening risks. 
This resilience is not unconditional. Kazakhstan and Uzbekistan need to preserve 
sufficiently attractive real rates to support demand for local assets, while Armenia 
remains dependent on external inflows and Azerbaijan on hydrocarbon revenues. A 
reversal of portfolio flows would test currencies and protection from imported inflation, 
particularly where external or fiscal deficits remain sizeable. Geopolitics is another 
source of risk; tighter sanctions enforcement, greater scrutiny of regional trade and 
financial flows, or pressure from larger neighbours could reduce the benefits the region 
has drawn from fragmentation. 
The individual country stories increasingly reflect different combinations of these 
strengths and vulnerabilities. Armenia continues to benefit from remittances, tourism 
and capital inflows, but twin deficits, Russia exposure and an expensive dram leave less 
room for disappointment. Azerbaijan has the region’s strongest sovereign balance sheet 
and substantial protection from higher oil prices, although declining oil production and 
limited private sector diversification constrain medium-term growth. Kazakhstan 
combines strong sovereign assets, high real rates and improving international market 
access, but persistent twin deficits and growing reliance on portfolio inflows make the 
tenge more sensitive to global positioning. 
Kyrgyzstan, the newest addition to our coverage, represents the region’s most dynamic 
story. Infrastructure investment, construction, trade and rapid credit expansion have 
produced exceptional growth, stronger revenues and improving headline debt ratios. Yet 
double-digit growth increasingly resembles overheating; inflation is above target, real 
rates are low, monetary transmission is weak and external accounts remain difficult to 
interpret. Large trade deficits, dependence on Russia-linked remittances, opaque 
financial flows and growing state and SOE-related investment obligations mean that 
macro credibility still needs to catch up with headline performance. 
Uzbekistan remains our preferred medium-term growth and reform story. Privatisation, 
high real rates and capital inflows support the soum and the expanding investable 
universe. However, overheating, twin deficits and depleted liquid sovereign-fund buffers 
prevent us from turning near-term currency resilience into a structural appreciation call.  
The country pages examine these increasingly distinct growth, policy and external-
balance stories in greater detail.

Economic overview  
Armenia has remained resilient to the global and regional challenges of 2026, 
supported by renewed inflows of visitors, remittances and capital. Foreign-policy 
developments and solid finances provide additional support to sentiment and the 
balance of payments. However, this resilience increasingly relies on continued 
external inflows, while persistent twin deficits and close economic links with Russia 
remain important vulnerabilities. Strong domestic demand and external cost 
pressures have also pushed inflation above target and prompted the region’s first 
rate hikes this year.  
While we remain constructive on Armenia’s near-term macro outlook and assets, 
growing exposure to foreign political and flow-related risks, alongside an 
increasingly expensive AMD, reduces the margin for error. 
Economic activity supported by the new wave of external inflows  
GDP growth remains elevated at c.7% YoY on a 4Q rolling basis. Some softening in 
investment-heavy industries in 9M26 has been offset by stronger activity in 
consumption, financial services and real estate. Armenia is benefiting from additional 
visitor arrivals and financial inflows, with net remittances rising from a typical c.6% to 
c.8-9% of GDP.  
Around two-thirds of gross remittance inflows originate in Russia, making it a key 
variable for Armenia’s growth outlook. Assuming foreign policy continuity, gradual 
moderation of GDP growth towards c.5% pa, alongside a fiscal deficit, remains our base 
case. A material disruption to Russia-related flows of people, goods (including Russia’s 
parallel imports) and capital remains a downside risk and a factor to watch. The 
medium-term question is whether improving regional connectivity and Western 
investment can increasingly replace the exceptional Russia-linked inflows of recent 
years as a structural growth driver. 
Fig 10 Armenia: GDP growth components by production  Fig 11 Armenia: GDP growth vs remittances

Source: National sources, CEIC, ING  Source: National sources, CEIC, ING

Household activity appears well supported by a combination of growing income, also 
thanks to remittances, fast lending growth and some rebound in state spending. 
Meanwhile, corporate activity is more subdued in 2026 compared to the 2025 spike as it 
remains heavily correlated with foreign trade and is subject to volatile regional politics. 
Russia still accounts for around one-third of Armenia's exports and imports, leaving 
corporate activity exposed to changes in bilateral relations.

Fiscal policy: Still supportive despite some recent consolidation 
The trailing budget deficit has narrowed to around 2.5% of GDP from 3.7% in the 
previous two years. Revenues have outperformed, mainly thanks to strong collection of 
consumption-related and corporate profit taxes, while spending growth has moderated, 
particularly in defence. We expect the deficit to gravitate back towards 3% of GDP over 
the medium term, but the commitment to overall fiscal discipline appears secure. 
Persistent deficits imply a steady issuance pipeline. We continue to expect the 
authorities to prioritise domestic borrowing to limit currency risk and develop local 
markets. Robust economic growth is improving debt sustainability despite rising nominal 
debt, leaving more room for potential external issuance as well. 
Fig 14 Armenia: Key budget parameters  Fig 15 Armenia: Key public debt parameters

Source: National sources, CEIC, ING  Source: National sources, CEIC, ING

Inflation and monetary policy: Growing CPI risks triggered a precautionary hike 
Inflation has hovered around 4% YoY for most of 2026. Relatively low import exposure to 
developed markets and c.5% YoY dram appreciation have helped contain price pressures 
from strong domestic demand and higher global commodity prices. Meanwhile, the 
deteriorating global inflation outlook, combined with regional and domestic risks, has 
raised concerns over further inflationary pressures among households, corporates and 
policymakers.  
With CPI likely to remain above the 3% target for longer, the Central Bank of Armenia 
made two consecutive 25bp hikes in September and November to 7.00%. We expect one 
further 25bp hike by year-end, although we do not see this developing into a prolonged 
hiking cycle.

Balance of payments and exchange rate: FX is supported, but increasingly expensive 
The dram has continued to show above-expected resilience to global capital volatility in 
2026, as it is supported by rising capital inflows, remittances, comfortable reserves and 
favourable investor sentiment linked to the peace process with Azerbaijan and positive 
rating trajectory. This continues to offset Armenia’s sizeable current account deficit, 
helping keep the currency stronger than implied by long-term valuation metrics.  
However, Armenia remains dependent on continued external flows, while close 
economic links to Russia remain vulnerabilities, even if the recent trade data points to a 
reduction in Russia-Armenia trade. Recent EU trade liberalisation covers around 80% of 
Armenian exports to the bloc. The immediate macro impact is likely to be limited, 
however, as the EU currently accounts for only around 12% of Armenia’s goods exports. 
We therefore expect the AMD to stay resilient to a potential global risk off in the near 
term but see limited room for further appreciation and continue to view longer-term 
risks as skewed towards gradual depreciation. 
Fig 18 Armenia: Trade balance level and composition  Fig 19 Armenia: Balance of payments vs AMD

Source: National sources, CEIC, ING  Source: National sources, CEIC, ING

Sovereign credit strategy 
This year has seen a continuation of fundamental improvement for Armenia, with 
positive rating outlooks at all three agencies, and elections that passed without major 
surprises. While there remains the potential for further positive catalysts from the 
ongoing peace process with Azerbaijan, conflict in the Middle East leaves Armenia in a 
more vulnerable position, given the increasing reliance on external inflows.

15

Economic overview  
Azerbaijan remains the CCA region’s financially strongest sovereign, supported by 
large and growing fiscal and monetary reserve assets, low public debt and a robust 
external balance sheet. Higher hydrocarbon prices have strengthened the near-
term fiscal and external outlook and supported confidence in the non-oil economy. 
However, the strength of the sovereign balance sheet contrasts with a much less 
impressive underlying growth story; declining oil production, limited diversification 
and subdued private investment continue to cap potential growth at around 2-3%. 
Inflation remains manageable for now, but imported price pressures warrant caution. 
We therefore remain constructive on sovereign resilience and AZN stability, while 
seeing the ability to convert hydrocarbon wealth into a deeper pool of private 
investment and investable non-oil assets as the main medium-term challenge. 
Economic activity: Recovering now, but long-term growth limited 
GDP growth is currently into the second year of below-average growth, dragged down 
by an underperforming fuel sector at the beginning of the year, reflecting both 
structural and temporary factors. At the same time, higher hydrocarbon prices support 
financial inflows, which pass through to the wider economy, supporting confidence in 
the non-fuel sectors. Retail trade and non-fuel industry output have been accelerating 
throughout 2026 amid faster lending activity.  
While the consolidated budget remains in surplus thanks to fuel revenues, the stable 
non-oil deficit supports domestic demand. Following the extended fuel-led slowdown of 
economic activity in 2025-26, we expect growth to recover in the medium term. 
However, the potential growth rate appears capped at 2-3% unless economic 
diversification accelerates. 
Fig 20 Azerbaijan: GDP growth by key contributors  Fig 21 Azerbaijan: GDP growth by sectors

Source: National sources, CEIC, ING  Source: National sources, CEIC, ING

Household spending relies increasingly on retail credit, as positive real income growth is 
insufficient to cover consumption. On the corporate side, the fuel sector still weighs on 
industrial growth, but the recent rise in corporate lending suggests investment appetite 
elsewhere may recover.

Budget policy is benefiting from higher oil price environment 
Azerbaijan’s fiscal stance remains robust, though not growth restrictive. On the revenue 
side, fuel receipts are broadly stable, as lower export volumes are being offset by higher 
prices. Non-fuel revenues are also largely stable. On the expenditure side, higher social 
and defence spending, alongside persistently high investment outlays, continue to 
underpin spending pressures. The war in neighbouring Iran creates upside risks to 
defence and security spending.  
As a result, the consolidated budget remains in a comfortable surplus of around 2% of 
GDP thanks to fuel revenues, while non-fuel budget is in stable c.10% GDP deficit. 
Azerbaijan’s fuel fundamentals remain solid, with annualised revenues per US$1/bbl of 
oil price at US$150-200 million. This has helped keep the fiscal breakeven oil price stable 
at around US$59/bbl last year, even as spending has increased. Even assuming some 
increase in the fiscal breakeven in the coming years due to continued spending growth, 
ING’s house view on oil prices suggests that Azerbaijan will remain in fiscal surplus on a 
three-year horizon. 
For investors, the bigger issue is asset creation. Strong public finances limit sovereign 
issuance, leaving the investment story dependent on new non-oil projects and private-
sector borrowers.  
Fig 24 Azerbaijan: Consolidated budget balance  Fig 25 Azerbaijan: Budget sensitivities to oil prices

Source: National sources, CEIC, ING  Source: National sources, CEIC, ING

Inflation and monetary policy: CPI risks manageable, rates on hold 
Inflation risks have recently shifted somewhat to the upside, as food prices and higher 
global transport and commodity costs have renewed pressure on Azerbaijan’s producer 
price index. However, the exchange-rate peg remains a strong nominal anchor, limiting 
pass-through to consumer prices. With CPI still within the 4±2% target range, we expect 
the refinancing rate to remain on hold over the medium term.

However, further imported inflation linked to developments in the Middle East is the

Balance of payments and AZN: External buffers reinforced by oil 
As on the fiscal side, higher oil prices have materially improved the external outlook, 
reversing much of the deterioration seen in 2025. As each US$10/bbl of oil price adds 
annual exports of around 4% of GDP, in 2026F the current account surplus may reach 
12% of GDP. In the meantime, sovereign assets equivalent to 115% of GDP keep growing 
and continue to provide substantial protection against potential external shocks. These 
buffers remain the main support for the AZN peg and broader macro stability.  
Over the longer term, however, declining hydrocarbon production and limited export 
diversification are likely to gradually erode the external surplus. Azerbaijan remains one 
of the region’s strongest external stories, although commodity dependence continues to 
shape its medium-term outlook. 
Fig 28 Azerbaijan: 12M exports by region  Fig 29 Azerbaijan: External sovereign asset position

Source: National sources, CEIC, ING  Source: National sources, CEIC, ING

Sovereign credit strategy  
The fundamental picture is generally positive for Azerbaijan, with ratings now 
comfortably in the IG space and Moody’s holding a positive outlook. At the same time, 
the country stands to be a significant beneficiary from the current energy price spike, 
given its high sensitivity to oil prices in terms of fiscal and external accounts. The 
ongoing peace process with Armenia seems more likely to act as a positive catalyst at 
this stage, given the progress in recent years.

### Page 18

CCA macro and credit November 2026

18

Economic overview 
Kazakhstan continues to benefit from one of the strongest sovereign and external 
positions in the CCA region, supported by sizeable sovereign assets, a balanced 
network of international partners and higher oil prices. Strong portfolio inflows, 
attractive real rates, progress towards Euroclear connectivity, growing domestic 
debt supply and positive rating momentum have supported the tenge and local 
markets, while a sizeable backlog of future state FX sales provides an additional 
buffer against market stress. 
However, these strengths coexist with persistent current-account deficits, continued 
fiscal and quasi-fiscal stimulus, limited private sector investment and an uneven 
track record in fiscal discipline and inflation targeting. Greater reliance on portfolio 
inflows is also making the tenge more sensitive to changes in global risk appetite. 
This creates a growing trade-off between the benefits of deeper foreign 
participation in the local market and greater sensitivity to shifts in global 
positioning. Current inflation trends leave room for moderate monetary easing, but 
domestic fiscal and quasi-fiscal stimulus alongside external price risks should keep 
the easing cycle gradual. 
Economic activity: growth resilient despite oil disruptions and VAT hike 
GDP growth has slowed this year compared to the exceptional pace seen in 2025 but 
remains robust despite the VAT hike and oil-related disruptions. Disruptions to oil 
production and exports appear to have been the main drag on growth in 1H26 and 
remain the main risk for 2H26.  
Non-oil sectors continue to perform relatively well, while higher oil prices support 
confidence and improve the fiscal and external outlook. Domestic demand also appears 
supported by quasi-fiscal stimulus estimated at 4-5% of GDP. At the same time, retail 
and corporate lending are showing signs of cooling amid tight monetary conditions. We 
see GDP growth remaining in the 4.5-5.0% range in the coming years. 
Fig 30 Kazakhstan: GDP growth dynamics and structure  Fig 31 Kazakhstan: Key oil sector indicators

Source: National sources, CEIC, ING  Source: National sources, CEIC, ING

Budget policy: Consolidation ongoing 
Headline fiscal policy has entered a consolidation phase, reflected in lower reliance on 
National Fund (NFRK) transfers to finance current spending, although a durable track 
record of fiscal discipline has yet to be established.

In addition to the quasi-fiscal stimulus provided through Baiterek lending, during 2026, 
the three-year state spending plan increased by KZT6.5tr to be carried out through 
targeted transfers from the National Fund (NFRK). This fits the previous pattern of 
spending plans being revised upwards over time. At the same time, even with the 
additional spending, the overall volume of National Fund spending, including guaranteed 
and targeted transfers, is still expected to decline, in our estimates, to 1.5-2.5% of GDP 
over the medium term.  
Importantly, however, this should have little effect on the headline republican (central 
government) budget deficit, which should remain in the 2-3% GDP range, determining 
the annual net debt issuance requirement. The consolidated budget should gravitate 
around balance but can also be subject to volatility due to the large role of NFRK’s 
investment income, which reached 4% of GDP in 2025. 
Fig 32 Kazakhstan: Republican budget balance  Fig 33 Kazakhstan: Consolidated budget balance

Source: National sources, CEIC, ING  Source: National sources, CEIC, ING

Inflation and monetary policy: Real rate to remain high 
The moderate easing in inflation from its 2025 peak and a resilient tenge have allowed 
the NBK to start cutting the base rate in 2026. Inflation pressures from the VAT hike and 
global commodity-price spike have remained under control for now, while portfolio 
inflows and attractive real yields have given an additional boost to the currency. At the 
same time, tariff increases, fiscal and quasi-fiscal stimulus, and producer-price pressures 
amid global inflation risks continue to justify our above-consensus CPI outlook.  
Therefore, we expect the easing cycle to continue only gradually, while remaining alert 
to potential external inflation shocks. Although Kazakhstan retains room for nominal 
easing, real policy rates are likely to remain high by regional standards and supportive 
of capital inflows.

Balance of payments and exchange rate: Portfolio flows increasingly matter 
Higher oil prices have strengthened Kazakhstan's external position, although part of the 
benefit is offset by weaker export volumes. Despite some recent improvement, the 
country continues to run structural current account deficits as strong imports and 
dividend outflows offset export revenues.  
Fig 36 Kazakhstan: Fuel export volume and value  Fig 37 Kazakhstan: Balance of payment composition

Source: National sources, CEIC, ING  Source: National sources, CEIC, ING

As a result, capital flows are becoming increasingly important for KZT dynamics. 
Progress towards Euroclear connectivity, positive rating momentum and additional 
public and corporate debt issuance should continue attracting portfolio inflows amid 
high real rates, supporting the tenge. Kazakhstan's capital market modernisation is also 
broadening beyond international settlement access, with the NBK now experimenting 
with tokenised debt issuance alongside the expanding digital tenge infrastructure. We 
have improved our KZT outlook but note that greater reliance on portfolio capital in an 
economy with persistent twin deficits also increases sensitivity to shifts in global risk 
appetite and flow reversals.  
The good news, however, is that the government has recently accumulated a backlog of 
FX sales linked to National Fund spending, which we estimate at c.US$2bn for June-
October. An unwinding of that backlog could provide an additional buffer for the 
domestic FX market in the event of external stress. 
Fig 38 Kazakhstan: Non-resident investments and KZT  Fig 39 Kazakhstan: NBRK/NFRK FX sales and KZT

Source: National sources, CEIC, ING  Source: National sources, CEIC, ING

Sovereign credit strategy 
Overall, the underlying fundamental momentum is generally positive, with fiscal 
consolidation under close focus, while higher energy prices should offer a modest boost 
to export and budget revenues. However, as with budget policy, on the issuance front, 
more quasi-sovereign supply remains a risk in the coming years given low spread levels 
compared to most BBB peers.

Economic overview  
Kyrgyzstan is the newest addition to our CCA coverage and arguably the region’s 
most striking growth story. Economic growth remains exceptionally strong, 
supported by construction and trade activity, infrastructure investment, remittance 
inflows and rapid credit growth. Public finances remain relatively comfortable and 
international reserves have risen sharply, while the stability of the som has helped 
insulate the economy from external price pressures. 
However, the current pace of growth also points to significant overheating risks. 
Inflation is already well above the central bank’s target range, credit expansion 
remains rapid and real rates offer little evidence of tight monetary conditions, while 
the recent slowdown in corporate deposit inflows points to greater volatility in the 
corporate funding base. Large external deficits, dependence on Russia-related 
remittances and unusually opaque external flows further complicate the picture. 
The recent sovereign rating improvement is encouraging, but Kyrgyzstan remains 
an early-stage catch-up story in which macro credibility is still lagging the headline 
growth. 
Economic activity: Exceptional growth, increasingly demand-led 
Despite recent moderation to 9.7% YoY as of 3Q26, economic growth in Kyrgyzstan 
remains exceptionally strong at 12.3% YoY on a rolling four-quarter basis, with trade and 
construction alone contributing around 9.6 percentage points. Construction has become 
a significantly more important growth engine since 2022, supported by infrastructure 
investment, while strong trade activity continues to reflect both domestic consumption 
and Kyrgyzstan's role in regional transit trade. Industrial performance, by contrast, 
remains more volatile. 
The composition of growth makes the current double-digit pace unlikely to be sustained 
indefinitely. Some of the exceptional post-2022 boost from regional trade rerouting 
should gradually fade, while activity is increasingly reliant on domestic investment and 
consumption. Large infrastructure projects should keep growth relatively strong, but the 
challenge is to convert the recent acceleration into broader productivity gains rather 
than a prolonged demand boom.

Fiscal policy: Headline surpluses mask a growing investment push 
Kyrgyzstan’s consolidated budget remains comfortably in surplus by 2.5-3.0% of GDP, 
supported by rapid economic growth and strong revenues, but the headline balance 
increasingly understates the expansionary direction of fiscal policy. Direct expenditures 
have been growing faster than GDP-driven tax revenues, largely reflecting the state’s 
infrastructure and investment push, as spending on ‘economic policies’ in the fiscal 
statistics is currently almost 5 percentage points of GDP higher than two years ago, 
eroding the underlying balance of tax revenues and direct expenditures. More 
importantly, once volatile non-tax revenues associated with financial and capital 
transactions are excluded, the fiscal position moves into a widening deficit. This helps 
explain why government borrowing has continued to increase despite the headline 
surplus. 
Nominal public debt has more than doubled from KGS436bn in 2021 to around 
KGS900bn currently, even as rapid nominal GDP growth has brought the debt ratio down 
sharply and more recently kept it broadly stable at around 40% of GDP. Recent 
borrowing, including the recent US$700m Eurobond placement in 2025, reflects 
primarily government financial transactions, including operations involving capital 
injections into state-owned enterprises, as suggested by the recent IMF analysis. The 
growing role of such operations means that the composition and use of new borrowing 
are becoming increasingly important for assessing Kyrgyzstan’s underlying fiscal 
position and sovereign credit story. 
Part of this borrowing reflects an infrastructure-heavy growth model, including the CKU 
railway and Kambarata-1. This raises potential growth if successfully executed, but also 
makes project execution, SOE balance sheets and the structure of external funding 
increasingly important for sovereign investors. 
Fig 42 Kyrgyzstan: Consolidated budget balance   Fig 43 Kyrgyzstan: Key public debt metrics

Source: National sources, IMF, CEIC, ING  Source: National sources, CEIC, ING

Inflation and monetary policy: Overheating sends hawkish signals 
Inflation has accelerated well beyond the National Bank's 5-7% target range. Domestic 
tariff increases were an important contributor in the first half of 2026, but price 
pressures have subsequently broadened, with strong demand adding to external food 
and other cost pressures. The relative stability of the som has provided an important 
offset to imported inflation. 
This leaves monetary policy in a difficult position. With inflation broadly matching the 
12% policy rate, monetary conditions do not look particularly tight. Rapid money and 
credit growth and abundant liquidity also point to weak transmission. We expect further 
near-term tightening, followed by scope for partial reversal during 2027 if inflation 
moderates. We also think stronger liquidity management, alongside the policy rate, will 
be important if the National Bank is to bring inflation sustainably back into its target 
range.

Balance of payments and KGS: Strong buffers, vulnerable external accounts 
Som stability masks a much more complicated external position. Kyrgyzstan runs a 
structurally large merchandise trade deficit, currently at US$11bn on a 12-month rolling 
basis, increasingly driven by machinery and other imports from China and other non-CIS 
economies. Some of these imports reflect the domestic investment boom, while part of 
the exceptional trade expansion has been related to regional re-exports. The latter 
appears to have peaked in 2024-2025 and has normalised since then. Meanwhile, trade 
ties with Russia remain deep, with bilateral trade exceeding US$4bn per annum, supporting 
near-term activity but also reinforcing Kyrgyzstan's exposure to Russia-related external risks. 
Remittance inflows provide an important counterweight to the trade deficit. Gross 
inflows remain close to record levels of US$3bn per annum and continue to be 
dominated by Russia (90%), while net remittances of around 11% of GDP provide 
substantial support to household incomes, domestic demand and the FX market. This 
provides a large buffer but leaves Kyrgyzstan heavily exposed to economic and policy 
developments in Russia. 
Fig 46 Kyrgyzstan: Trade balance by region  Fig 47 Kyrgyzstan: Net remittances

Source: National sources, CEIC, ING  Source: National sources, CEIC, ING

Official BOP data requires particular caution; IMF estimates suggest that unrecorded  
re-exports materially distort the measured current account deficit. Structural current 
account deficits of US$5-7bn per annum require continued external financing, while 
large unrecorded trade and financial flows make the headline current account numbers 
unusually difficult to interpret. FDI and, more recently, portfolio flows have helped 
finance the external funding gap. International reserves have risen sharply in nominal 
terms, but much of the increase reflects gold revaluation; physical gold holdings have 
remained broadly stable. 
For now, these buffers and the managed exchange rate regime should continue 
ensuring som stability, seen since mid-2025. But the combination of a large trade deficit,

Banking sector: Rapid financial deepening brings its own risks 
Loan growth in Kyrgyzstan remains very strong, particularly in retail, which is expanding 
rapidly from a relatively low base, though the pace has recently moderated. Corporate 
loan growth is accelerating and catching up with retail, with FX lending playing a stable 
role. Meanwhile, deposit and broader funding growth has slowed. Most notably, 
corporate deposit inflows slowed materially, and this stands out compared to other 
segments of the banking sector. 
The initial drop appears partly related to large corporate and government payments in the 
middle of the year, but the subsequent weakness may also reflect tighter sanctions 
compliance, including on the A7 payment network and the ruble-linked A757 
cryptocurrency, as well as new restrictions and fees affecting non-resident corporate 
accounts.   
On the lending side, the low starting point means that part of the expansion represents 
welcome financial deepening. However, rapid credit creation is also reinforcing domestic 
demand and weakening monetary policy transmission at a time when inflation is 
already high. Credit quality, bank liquidity and the pace at which lending continues to 
outgrow the funding base are therefore becoming increasingly important macro-
financial indicators to watch. 
Fig 50 Kyrgyzstan: Bank funding indicators  Fig 51 Kyrgyzstan: Bank lending indicators

Source: National sources, CEIC, ING  Source: National sources, CEIC, ING

Sovereign credit strategy 
While Kyrgyzstan remains an interesting reform story for EM investors, recent 
performance has seen spreads squeeze tight versus peers. Headline fiscal metrics look 
strong but mask increased spending and borrowing needs, which could drive further

Economic overview 
Uzbekistan remains our preferred medium-term CCA growth and reform story, but 
strong recent performance also brings increasing signs of overheating and leaves 
important macro imbalances unresolved. Growth remains well above potential, 
inflation is still above target and persistent twin deficits coexist with continued high 
state involvement in the economy and a much-reduced liquid component of the 
sovereign wealth fund. At the same time, reform and privatisation momentum, high 
real rates and strong capital inflows have supported the soum, while unusually 
restrained physical gold exports leave potential for additional FX supply if sales 
normalise. 
We therefore retain a stronger-than-consensus medium-term view on UZS and see 
scope for gradual monetary easing, but a durable break from its historical 
depreciation trend requires further disinflation, fiscal discipline and evidence that 
private capital inflows can remain strong as the current reform and privatisation 
push matures. 
Economic activity: Accelerating beyond potential 
Uzbekistan’s GDP growth has been exceptionally strong recently, supported by domestic 
demand catching up from a low base. While activity is expected to moderate from the 
current above-potential levels, Uzbekistan should remain one of the fastest-growing 
economies in emerging markets. Strong investment spending, industrial activity and 
ongoing reforms continue to support expansion, while positive demographic trends 
provide an additional tailwind.  
A gradual slowdown in the GDP growth is likely to reflect normalisation in the activity 
trends, with growth remaining comfortably above regional peers. The immediate 
challenge is managing overheating as consumption and investment continue to grow 
rapidly.  
Fig 53 Uzbekistan: GDP growth composition  Fig 54 Uzbekistan: Key activity indicators

Source: National sources, CEIC, ING  Source: National sources, CEIC, ING

Fiscal policy: Pace of consolidation moderated 
Fiscal consolidation is continuing, with the general government deficit narrowing to 1.1% 
of GDP in the four quarters to 3Q26, from 4.1% in 2023. The pace of improvement has 
slowed recently, partly because unusually low gold exports have constrained revenues, 
which we expect to be temporary. At the same time, expenditure growth is catching up 
with revenue growth, pointing to a more structural challenge for Uzbekistan’s fiscal policy.

The government also has strong incentives to maintain fiscal restraint. Domestic 
demand is already running hot, while several years of budget deficits have reduced the 
UFRD's liquid component from 17% of GDP in 2019 to around 3% in 2025. We see fiscal 
deficits limited to around 1% of GDP on the three-year horizon, with a growing share of 
debt financing and privatisation as a source of financing, which should be taken 
positively by portfolio investors. 
The privatisation programme, though stemming largely from the fiscal needs, is gaining 
importance as a capital-markets story. Following the UzNIF transaction, the widening 
pipeline of state assets creates scope for international capital raising. 
Fig 55 Uzbekistan: Consolidated budget balance   Fig 56 Uzbekistan: Sovereign fund composition

Source: National sources, IMF, CEIC, ING  Source: National sources, IMF, Fitch, CEIC, ING

Inflation and monetary policy: Scope for gradual easing 
Over the past couple of years, inflation in Uzbekistan has moderated substantially from 
previous highs, aided by the recent fiscal consolidation, and this has created room for a 
cautious monetary policy easing cycle. However, current CPI remains above the central 
bank's long-term objective, while strong domestic demand, administrative tariff 
adjustments and global food and commodity risks argue against aggressive rate cuts.  
We therefore expect the central bank to deliver only gradual easing from a still-
restrictive level. Real rates remain relatively attractive and should continue to support 
macro stability while allowing policymakers to balance growth objectives against the 
need for further disinflation. 
Fig 57 Uzbekistan: Inflation, expectations, and key rate  Fig 58 Uzbekistan: Inflation drivers

Source: National sources, CEIC, ING  Source: National sources, CEIC, UN FAO, ING

Our stronger-than-consensus medium-term view on the soum continues to be 
reinforced by recent developments. High real rates, gold prices, an expected catch-up in 
physical gold exports and growing foreign participation in Uzbekistan's privatisation 
programme provide important support for the balance of payments and FX market. The 
investment pipeline continues to broaden geographically, with a recently announced

package of US-linked projects potentially worth US$12bn upon implementation, spanning 
transport, critical minerals, infrastructure and financial services. This should underpin the 
resilience of the UZS despite the country's persistent trade and current-account deficits.  
Uzbekistan’s physical gold exports have been significantly restrained this year, at just 18 
tonnes in January-September 2026 compared with 85 tonnes in the same period of 
2025. This means that the benefit from the gold price rally has so far been limited on the 
external trade side. The slight appreciation of the soum against major currencies this 
year appears to have been driven largely by capital inflows, partly supported by high 
real rates, as has been the case in Kazakhstan. 
Fig 59 Uzbekistan: CBRU monthly gold exports  Fig 60 Uzbekistan: Balance of payments

Source: National sources, CEIC, ING  Source: National sources, CEIC, ING

The reason for the unusually low sales has not been formally explained. However, the 
simultaneous increase in the central bank's physical gold holdings – to 453.5 tonnes in 
October - indicates deliberate retention amid continued extraction. The significant 
restraint on gold exports so far this year suggests some upside potential for the soum if 
export volumes recover towards their historical range of 80-120 tonnes per year. This 
catch-up in delayed gold exports is one of the factors we see supporting the currency 
over the medium term. 
However, the longer-term outlook is less certain, as Uzbekistan continues to run sizeable 
twin deficits, while inflation remains above target. As a result, we view the UZS positively 
in the medium-term but believe a durable break from the historical depreciation trend 
would require sustained progress in reducing macro imbalances and deepening 
structural reforms. 
Sovereign credit strategy 
The fundamental picture for Uzbekistan has improved significantly in recent years, with 
narrowing twin deficits amid solid growth reflected in rating upgrades. Fitch’s positive 
outlook suggests there is still scope for improvement to continue, while the stronger 
credit profile should increase the country’s resilience to the current energy shock. An 
expected catch-up in gold exports, alongside ongoing capital inflows linked to the 
privatisation pipeline should keep the momentum strong, and we see the potential for 
more fundamental upside.

## Document 323: Double Feature_ The Spy Who Loved Me & To China With Love

Source PDF: all report pdf/Double Feature_ The Spy Who Loved Me & To China With Love.pdf
Report source: researchmail轉寄
Date: 2026-11-13
Pages: 8
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

From:    Yardeni QuickTakes <yardeni-research@ghost.io>
Subject: Double Feature: The Spy Who Loved Me & To China With Love
Date:    8 hours ago
Double Feature: The Spy Who Loved Me & To China With Love

YARDENI QUICKTAKES
Double Feature: The Spy Who
Loved Me & To China With Love
By Ed Yardeni & Elias Griepentrog • 12 Nov 2026
View in browser
Photo by Vows on the Move / Unsplash

I. Jensen Versus The AI Trade
Charlie Munger famously warned that there are three ways smart men can
go broke: "Liquor, ladies, and leverage." NVIDIA co-founder and now
former CEO Jensen Huang has run into trouble with the second of
Munger's three L's. He won't go broke, but he is out of a good job.
On Thursday, NVIDIA announced that Huang had resigned as CEO after
33 years at the helm of the company he co-founded in 1993. His

### Page 2

departure followed revelations of extramarital affairs, including a reported
relationship with a Chinese businesswoman suspected by US intelligence
officials of spying for Beijing. Her Singapore-based company, Megaspeed,
allegedly purchased NVIDIA's high-powered GPU chips and re-exported
them to China, violating US export restrictions. The longtime CFO Colette
Kress was appointed interim CEO while the board searches for a
successor.
Huang's departure rattled the AI trade, with NVIDIA shares plunging 4.3%,

sending its forward P/E to 11.7 (charts). We promised you buying
opportunities after the midterm elections. There were a bunch last
Thursday and a few more today.

### Page 3

Competitors AMD and Broadcom advanced 5.9% and 5.5%, respectively,
on expectations of market-share gains (chart)
As a result, the Nasdaq Composite held up relatively well, falling just 0.4%
today (chart).
Neoclouds also tumbled, with CoreWeave down 6.7% and Nebius down
16.8%, amid concerns about NVIDIA-backed financing (chart). Nebius
was additionally pressured by disappointing revenue guidance and rising
capital spending.

### Page 4

Despite the latest AI jitters, the two-year Treasury yield rose 15bps to
4.87% this evening, remaining well above the federal funds rate and
signaling that further Fed tightening is warranted (chart). The 10-year
Treasury yield is back above 5.00% at 5.13% this evening.
Yields rose on higher oil prices, with Brent crude up 3.1% to $95.66 this
evening, after President Donald Trump dismissed the need for an Iran
deal, and on hawkish Fed commentary following October's hot CPI report

(chart).

### Page 5

Markets now price a 100% probability of a December Fed rate hike:
84.7% for 25bps and 15.3% for 50bps. Futures imply more than 3 rate
hikes over the next 12 months (chart).
The combination of higher Treasury yields, driven by rising oil prices and
hawkish Fed commentary, and renewed AI jitters weighed on the broader
stock market. The S&P 500 fell 0.4%, its equal-weighted counterpart
declined 0.9%, and the Russell 2000 lost 1.7% (charts).

### Page 6

II. Nvidia: The Bear Case
We can assess the risks Jensen Huang's departure poses to the broader
AI trade with our familiar framework of three constraints on the AI
investment boom.
(1) Financial constraints. NVIDIA has been described by many as the
central bank of the AI trade, conducting its own version of quantitative
easing by helping finance the expansion of AI computing capacity. The
company spent $42.2 billion purchasing equity securities during the first

half of its current fiscal year, compared with just $1.2 billion a year earlier.
The fear is that Huang's departure could slow or even end these financing
activities, tightening financial conditions for an industry already facing
questions about the returns on its enormous capital expenditures.

### Page 7

(2) Political constraints. Huang has also been the AI trade's biggest
cheerleader, tirelessly promoting its economic benefits and defending key
players. During October's AI jitters, for example, he publicly defended
CoreWeave's financial health amid a selloff in its shares and bonds,
helping restore confidence. His departure could deprive the industry of its
most influential advocate and cheerleader.
Additionally, NVIDIA reportedly intervened in the Commerce Department's

investigation into Megaspeed, while Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick
reportedly resisted efforts to blacklist the company, which remains off the
Entity List. Reports also suggest the Trump administration knew about
Huang's relationship with a suspected Chinese spy. These allegations
could invite tougher congressional oversight and undermine Trump's
national security argument for building more data centers in the US,
making the AI buildout harder to sell to the public.

(3) Physical constraints. Washington could respond by tightening chip
export controls and closing loopholes that allow Chinese companies to
access NVIDIA-powered computing capacity remotely. If so, China might
retaliate by restricting exports of rare earth minerals, some of which are
crucial for building data centers. China has already warned Washington
against using the controversy as a pretext for imposing tougher
technology restrictions.
III. Nvidia: Our Base Case

Our base case is that NVIDIA's financing strategy remains intact. Colette
Kress, NVIDIA's longtime CFO and newly appointed interim CEO, has
worked closely with Huang since 2013 and helped oversee the company's
strategic investments and financing arrangements. During NVIDIA's
August 26 earnings call, she defended these investments against
accusations of circular financing, stating, "We recognize the scale of this
support, and we know some will call it circular financing. We see it

differently." She argued that these investments would generate attractive
returns while strengthening NVIDIA's AI ecosystem, suggesting continuity
under her leadership. We don't expect political constraints to meaningfully
increase as a result of the controversy. Nor do we expect another trade
conflict with China to erupt.

### Page 8

Even if some of the constraints intensify, they would likely turn existing
speed bumps into bigger speed bumps rather than roadblocks to the AI
investment boom. AI remains the latest evolution in the Digital Revolution
that began with IBM's mainframes, and we expect the investment boom to
continue without Huang (chart). We therefore view any resulting selloff as
another buying opportunity for the AI trade.

### Page 4

Monthly Economics
Sector Analysis
Personal Consumption Expenditures (PCE)
• We've lifted our Q3 expectation for real PCE on incoming data and a slightly firmer consumer
backdrop in the wake of recent revisions.
Consumer spending looks firmer in the third quarter, and we now expect real PCE to rise at a 3.4%
annualized clip. This upward revision reflects recent data, with August consumption coming in firm
with real spending rising by the fastest monthly clip in nearly two years, but also reflects a slightly

firmer consumer backdrop in the wake of the BEA's annual revisions released late September. Although
revisions did not materially alter spending, they lifted the level of household income in recent years,
which also boosted the personal saving rate. The trajectory of real income growth remains weak, but
the consumer still looks to be in a slightly stronger financial position than previously believed, which
helps explain recent resilience in spending and suggests consumers may have a bit more runway ahead.

Investment: Equipment, Intellectual Property Products and Inventories
• The AI build out will lead to another surge in business fixed investment spending in Q3, though the
effect on headline growth will be partly offset by stronger imports.
Business fixed investment spending continues to be supported primarily by the high-tech buildout.
We forecast real equipment investment to rise at around a 16% annualized clip in Q3, but part of
this boost to headline GDP growth will be offset by stronger import growth that is facilitating the

build out. Net imports of computers are tracking to surge in the third quarter, which will provide a
sizable lift to information processing equipment investment, or around half of overall equipment.
And while non-defense capital goods shipments (including aircraft) slipped in August, the near-term
growth trajectory remains supportive of a pickup in equipment. The rebuild in inventories we have
been anticipating is also tracking to be a bit stronger as businesses replenish drawn down product.

Investment: Residential
• The abrupt rise in mortgage rates is likely to dampen residential investment over the next few
quarters. Residential spending should find support next year as longer-term interest rates
moderate somewhat.
We are unlikely to see another positive residential investment reading in Q3. Rising mortgage rates

have sent a chill over builder outlooks, pushing sales expectations to their weakest level in more than
three years. While builders have responded with deeper price cuts, affordability pressures continue
to weigh on single-family sales and construction outlays. Apartment construction is simultaneously
soft amid rising financing costs and modest rent growth, and home improvement spending remains
constrained by high materials costs and interest rates. While residential investment is likely to remain

4 | Economics

### Page 5

Economic Outlook: October 2026 Economics
a drag this year, we expect a slight moderation in longer-term interest rates in 2027, which would
provide a more supportive backdrop.
Investment: Nonresidential Structures
• We have not made material changes to our nonresidential structures forecast. Despite elevated
interest rates, data center and related spending should continue to support structures investment
through our forecast horizon.
Nonresidential structures spending has accelerated sharply, overwhelmingly driven by data centers

and electric power facilities. As data center construction expands geographically and demand rises
for semiconductors, high-tech capex has also started to generate positive spillovers for warehousing
and manufacturing construction. This buildout, which appears relatively insensitive to interest rates,
should be enough to sustain solid growth in structures investment even as spending across most
other categories remains essentially flat. Domestic energy is another green shoot, with elevated prices

supporting ongoing growth in active rotary rigs.

Net Exports & Trade
• An expected drag from trade in Q3 got larger in light of a near-record trade deficit in August, net
exports is now expected to lop off three full percentage points from third quarter growth.

Exports increased in August for the first time in four months, but the increase was more than offset by
an even larger increase in imports. The upshot was a widening in the trade deficit to a near-record gap
of more than $105 billion. Going into the report, we had a 1.9 percentage point drag from net exports
penciled in for Q3 GDP; we revised that to a full 3.0 percentage points drag in light of the record gap.
Even a modest narrowing in the final months of the year would likely mean a lift to headline GDP in

Q4, but the real story here is that trade will remain a headwind throughout the forecast period owing
largely to surging imports of tech-related products amid the AI boom.
Labor Market
• The jobs market remains stable. We continue to expect the unemployment rate to end the year at
4.2% and hold near that level in 2027.
We modestly revised down our near-term payroll growth forecast following the September jobs
report, but otherwise we have left our labor market outlook unchanged. Measures of labor demand

growth continue to point to a stable labor market. Job openings have inched higher on a year-ago
basis but remain broadly steady while initial jobless claims, WARN notices and Challenger job cut
announcements continue to signal low layoffs. Labor force participation appears to be improving after
its sharp pullback in the first half of the year. Looking ahead, we expect payroll growth to average
around 60K per month in 2027, roughly in line with our estimate of breakeven job growth. That should

Economics | 5

### Page 6

Monthly Economics
keep the unemployment rate near 4.2% in 2027 and leave labor market conditions broadly consistent
with "full employment." Notably, our expectation for labor cost growth of ~3.4% is the reason we
continue to reiterate that labor cost growth is not a contributor to the excess inflation seen in the
data.
Inflation
• We expect the core PCE deflator to rise 3.2% and 2.6% Q4/Q4 in 2026 and 2027, respectively.
Headline inflation remains heavily influenced by developments in the Middle East, with higher oil

prices lifting gasoline and diesel prices. Food inflation has been tame this year but looks poised to
strengthen as higher transportation and commodity prices work their way through supply chains.
Beyond food and energy, price pressures remain concentrated in a handful of categories, most notably
airfares and AI-related tech goods. Core goods inflation is showing signs of stabilizing, as waning tariff-
related price pressures have been offset by resilient demand tied to the AI buildout. We expect some

improvement here in 2027. Still-low shelter inflation and cooler price growth for airfares and health
care should help bring about some moderation in core services inflation next year. We expect the core
PCE deflator to rise 3.2% on a Q4-over-Q4 basis in 2026 before slowing to 2.6% in 2027.

Fiscal Policy
• Fiscal year (FY) 2026 ended on September 30, and the federal government tallied a $2.0 trillion
budget deficit (6.5% of GDP). We project the deficit to be unchanged in dollar terms in FY 2027

and a bit smaller as a share of GDP (~6%).
• Another continuing resolution to extend government funding past the December 11 deadline
seems like the most likely outcome at this time for the FY 2027 budget. It is possible Republicans
use the lame duck session of Congress to increase defense spending and raise the debt ceiling.
Looking ahead to the lame duck session of Congress that will follow the midterm elections, Congress
needs to pass a bill that extends government funding past December 11 to avert a government

shutdown. Another continuing resolution that punts the issue into Q1 seems like the most likely
outcome. Perhaps more interesting will be whether Republicans attempt to use budget reconciliation
to pass one more partisan piece of legislation. If they do, we suspect the two things at the top the
agenda will be more funding for national defense and immigration enforcement priorities as well as a
debt ceiling increase. For the latter, raising it in the lame duck would remove some leverage Democrats

could have in 2027 if they recapture one or both chambers. It is still very early, but we estimate the
debt ceiling "X date" by which Congress must act or face default is sometime in late Q3/early Q4 of
next year.

### Page 7

Economic Outlook: October 2026 Economics
Monetary Policy & Interest Rates
• Our base case forecast is for the FOMC to hike the fed funds rate by 25 bps in December and
then keep the policy rate unchanged in 2027 at 4.00%-4.25%, with risks skewed toward additional
tightening.
As we go to print, the FOMC seems more likely than not to keep the fed funds rate unchanged in
October. But given the rationale under which the Fed started this tightening cycle and the forcefulness

with which Warsh has driven home his displeasure with elevated inflation, it seems entirely plausible
to us that October will be a live meeting. At a minimum, the recent inflation data seem to make a hike
in December a lock. Past December, the outlook will be highly dependent on future inflation data. Our
expectation is that core inflation will cool gradually in 2027, and we still do not see signs of demand-
side inflation in the data. But hiking cycles have an inertial component to them, and the recent inflation

data reset the clock to zero on the Fed seeing several good inflation prints in a row. This creates upside
risk to our base case for the fed funds rate.

International Developments
• Global growth remains resilient, leaving our 2026 and 2027 forecasts unchanged. Our inflation
forecasts are also unchanged, although risks of delayed price pass-through could prolong inflation
pressures into 2027.

• Monetary policy continues to lean toward tightening across advanced economies, while emerging
market central banks are increasingly cautious, either holding rates or easing more gradually as
inflation pressures rebuild.
The balance of risks to global inflation remains tilted to the upside, complicating the monetary policy
outlook even as growth has proven more resilient than expected. Elevated energy prices, particularly
for refined petroleum products, persistent geopolitical uncertainty and El Niño-related food price

risks could prolong inflationary pressures into 2027. While second-round effects still remain relatively
contained, repeated supply shocks are testing central banks’ willingness to look through the temporary
price increases, particularly where inflation expectations remain elevated, even as higher government
bond yields are already contributing to tighter financial conditions.
Across advanced economies, central banks face increasingly difficult trade-offs as persistent inflation

pressures limit the scope for policy accommodation. The Eurozone remains particularly vulnerable
to energy supply disruptions, with elevated natural gas prices and low storage levels adding to
inflation risks. We continue to expect a 25 bps European Central Bank hike in December, followed by a
prolonged hold through 2027. Fiscal concerns are also adding to pressure, particularly in France, where
the selloff in government debt has pushed yields higher. In the U.K., resilient activity and renewed

Economics | 7

### Page 8

Monthly Economics
inflation pressures similarly underpin our expectation for a Bank of England hike in November,
followed by a hold through 2027.
Elsewhere, policy paths are becoming more differentiated. After four hikes this year brought the Cash
Rate to 4.60%, we expect the Reserve Bank of Australia to remain on hold, although renewed inflation
pressures or stronger-than-expected activity could prompt further tightening. We also still expect
the Bank of Canada to hold at 2.25%, with subdued growth weighing against energy-driven inflation

risks, although incoming inflation data over the coming weeks will be critical in determining whether
persistent price pressures warrant tightening monetary policy perhaps as early as the December
meeting. Japan remains an exception to the broader hold outlook: while the yen's recovery provides
some relief from imported inflation, elevated energy prices and import dependence continue to pose
upside risks. We expect the Bank of Japan to hike again by year-end and now expect an additional 25

bps hike in Q3-2027, bringing the terminal rate to 1.75%.
The emerging market story has increasingly shifted from easing toward patience or selective
tightening. In Asia, China's weak domestic demand and persistent property-sector strains continue to
warrant policy support, with AI-related exports providing some offset. We expect a more gradual pace
of Reserve Requirement Ratio cuts alongside targeted monetary and fiscal measures. India faces the

opposite challenge, with elevated food and energy prices and rupee depreciation strengthening the
case for further Reserve Bank of India tightening. In Latin America, peso depreciation and elevated
energy costs pose upside inflation risks for Mexico, although limited exchange-rate pass-through
should allow Banxico to remain on hold while policymakers monitor underlying inflation and inflation
expectations. In Brazil, election-related uncertainty and renewed inflation pressures are likely to delay

further easing, with cuts expected to resume in Q1-2027 once policymakers see more convincing
evidence of disinflation.

## Document 338: Eurozone_ Improving business sentiment, driven by tech, is spreading to other sectors (2026-10-14)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Eurozone_ Improving business sentiment, driven by tech, is spreading to other sectors (2026-10-14).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-10-14
Pages: 3
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

14 October 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
EUROZONE: IMPROVING BUSINESS SENTIMENT, DRIVEN BY TECH, IS SPREADING TO OTHER SECTORS
Stéphane Colliac & Tarik Rharrab
*LATEST OBSERVATION: 5 OCTOBER 2026  **ESTIMATE
The September PMI surveys confirm that growth in the Eurozone is getting stronger, boosted in particular by new export orders, which are
still rising. Sectors that were already performing well – technology and capital goods (including for defence) – are continuing to grow, while

those that had been struggling until now – aeronautics, consumer goods and financial services – are seeing their production indices recover.
This strong performance is particularly noteworthy, given that it has not yet been undermined by higher inflation.
The heatmap shows the PMI index readings: a reading above 50 indicates expansion, while a reading below 50 signals contraction compared to the previous
month. For each indicator, sectors are ranked in descending order based on their September 2026 reading.

Shades of red represent values above 50, while shades of blue represent values below 50: the intensity of the colour increases the further away they are
from this threshold.
Production growth spreads to more sectors
The PMI surveys on business sentiment confirm that business activity in the Eurozone is expanding at a faster pace and spreading to more sectors.
Household-consumption-related sectors (goods and services), which were adversely affected in the spring by the outbreak of the conflict in the Middle

East, do not appear to be negatively impacted by the rising inflation observed since August. The aeronautics sector, where production has mainly been
limited by supply constraints, is also showing signs of improvement.
SOURCE: S&P GLOBAL, BNP PARIBAS

Chart of the Week / 14 October 2026 economic-research.bnpparibas.com
These trends, although still moderate, come alongside a drive to upgrade equipment, fuelled by investment in digital technology, electrification
(which is supporting the automotive sector) and defence. The momentum in technology goods and services further strengthened in September.
The production index for computer and electronics hit a five-year high, while the software services production index stayed close to its July peak.

Financial services are also enjoying a significant improvement, reflecting the healthy lending momentum despite interest rates starting to rise
(see our analysis).
Growth driven by exports
Exports drove growth in the first half of 2026, both across the Eurozone as a whole (see our analysis) and among its key members (notably
Germany). This momentum is also increasing, as indicated by the ‘new export orders’ component of the various PMI indices. As was the case with

production, there has been a particularly noteworthy increase for capital goods (technology and machinery) and related services, particularly digital
services. This trend is playing out more broadly, as, in September, new export orders rose across all sub-sectors, with the exception of intermediate
goods and consumer goods, where contraction recently eased. There has been a significant improvement in the transport sector, particularly in
the aeronautics industry, which has major markets in Asia. Recent data on French exports show that aeronautics exports to this region are rising

once again.
The convergence between rising production and increasing export-order backlogs shows that Eurozone growth is based on an increasingly solid
foundation. Inflationary pressures are not holding it back for the time being.

## Document 342: First Impressions_ NZ labour market surveys, September quarter 2026 (2026-11-17)

Source PDF: all report pdf/First Impressions_ NZ labour market surveys, September quarter 2026 (2026-11-17).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-11-17
Pages: 10
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

#labour #newzealand
First Impressions: NZ labour market surveys, September quarter
2026
By Michael Gordon
Senior Economist, Westpac NZ
09:01 November 04 2026 Share
The unemployment rate rose to 5.7% in the September quarter, against expectations for a
small decline. Spare capacity in the labour market is keeping wage pressures subdued.
Unemployment rate: 5.7% (prev: 5.6%, Westpac: 5.5%, RBNZ: 5.5%, mkt: 5.5%)
Employment change: -0.1% (prev: +0.3%, Westpac: +0.5%, RBNZ: +0.3%, mkt: +0.4%)

### Page 2

Participation rate: 70.4% (prev: 70.6%, Westpac: 70.8%, RBNZ: 70.6%, mkt: 70.7%) 
Labour costs (private sector): +0.5% (prev: +0.7%, Westpac: +0.5%, RBNZ: +0.4%, mkt: +0.5%)

The September quarter surveys pointed to ongoing softness in New Zealand’s labour market. The
unemployment rate rose from 5.6% to 5.7%, continuing an almost five-year run of increases.
Employment was softer than expected, and wage growth remains subdued despite increasing cost-of-
living pressures. 
The Household Labour Force Survey (HLFS) showed a surprise 0.1% fall in employment in the
September quarter, after a downwardly revised 0.3% increase in the June quarter. That went against
the Monthly Employment Indicator (MEI) measure of filled jobs, which had suggested some upward
momentum emerging in the jobs market (and notably, the HLFS measure had outpaced the MEI for the
previous nine quarters). It was also softer than suggested by the business-focused Quarterly
Employment Survey (QES), which recorded a 0.2% rise in full-time equivalent employees, a 0.1% rise
in filled jobs and a 0.6% rise in hours paid. 
The fall in HLFS employment was accompanied by a fall in labour force participation rate, from 70.6%
to 70.4%. These co-movements are effectively a reversal of the surprisingly large increases that we
saw in the June quarter, which we noted at the time were indicative of sampling error in the survey. 
This is why we generally recommend focusing on the unemployment rate, as it’s less vulnerable to
sampling error. And in that respect, the message on the jobs market remains weak: to the extent that
employment may be rising, it’s still not fast enough to absorb the growth in the population (which is
itself a fairly low bar, as net migration remains at a relatively low ebb). 
The HLFS also recorded a sharp 1.6% fall in hours worked (although usual hours worked were down
just 0.4%). This seems to have been influenced by Matariki – a floating holiday that was only
introduced a few years ago, so will not be picked up by the seasonal adjustment. This may have also
been responsible for a marked shift from full-time to part-time employment, as some people may have
been pushed into the part-time category simply by virtue of only working four days during the survey
week. 
The Labour Cost Index (LCI) rose by 0.5% in the September quarter, in line with our expectations
overall but a bit weaker in the details – private sector wages were up by 0.5%, while there was a
surprisingly large 0.7% increase in public sector wages. There was a notable jump in the number of
employers citing cost-of-living adjustments as a reason for increasing pay rates, although the size of
those increases varied a great deal, with many substantially less than the headline inflation rate. 
Overall, we think the details are soft from the RBNZ’s perspective – an important consideration ahead
of the 9 December Monetary Policy Statement, as there are few other major data releases between
now and then. The unemployment rate is a more reliable signal than the employment measures, and
the ongoing rise in unemployment points to more slack in the labour market than the RBNZ was hoping
to see in an economy that has been growing for over a year now. The doves on the policy committee
will be encouraged in their view that there is limited scope for headline inflation shocks to pass through
into second-round effects. 
Michael Gordon, Senior Economist
P: +64 21 749 506

1.26元 EPS。3Q26營收及毛利率低於我們預期，主要因磷化銦基板供
應仍受限制，德系基板供應商產能爬坡進展不如預期，而主要日系供
應商供貨份額未有太大改變，使高毛利磷化銦產品營收佔比自前季
48.7%下滑至 33.4%，且砷化鎵產品營收占比因相關射頻及國防訂單顯
著上升，影響產品組合。3Q26營業費用 0.65億元大約符合我們預期，
因公司持續投注研發費用於專業機台進行較高階產品的研發，費用較
往年明顯提升。 
磷化銦磊晶片仍是 2027年主要成長動能：2027年成長動能仍來自磷
化銦磊晶片，雖 3Q26供應狀況未如我們先前預期，公司認為磷化銦
基板供應於 4Q26已有些微放緩跡象，展望 2027年供應限制有望進一
步改善，預期毛利率將回升至四成以上水準。2027年公司展望營收成
長約 10%至 25%，新興 APD及 HBT磊晶產品也預計於 2027年量產，
而除磷化銦外，4Q26起砷化鎵及銻化鎵國防與商用訂單顯著成長，其
中銻化鎵紅外磊晶片已加速訂單需求，預期 2027 年訂單持續擴大，
並增加產能。磷化銦基板供應未如先前所預期顯著改善以及公司明年
展望狀況，我們下修 2027年營收及獲利預估。 
砷化鎵光電產品靜待多點開花：為降低對傳統磷化銦基板供應的依
賴，公司積極開發 QD Laser、變晶型 PIN等新產品切入光通訊市場，
其中，QD Laser 已受到客戶初步良好反饋，進行送樣當中。 200G 
VCSEL方面也持續進行研發，並已與客戶配合 NRE。 
受股數稀釋獲利及市場影響下調評價：公司與光聖進行股份交換使流
通在外股數增加致流通在外股數上升，我們預估 2026、2027年 EPS 
分別為 5.08、7.66元，此外，考量近期市場評價有較大幅度修正，較
前次報告下調本益比，我們基於 2027預估 EPS x 50倍本益比，目標
價為 383元，調降至中立之投資評等建議。風險因子：基板供應短缺。

## Document 392: QE速報：2026年7-9月期の実質ＧＤＰは前期比▲0.0％（年率▲0.1％）－民間消費の落ち込みを主因として4四半期ぶりのマイナス成長

Source PDF: all report pdf/QE速報：2026年7-9月期の実質ＧＤＰは前期比▲0.0％（年率▲0.1％）－民間消費の落ち込みを主因として4四半期ぶりのマイナス成長.pdf

（前期比、％）
（年・四半期）
実質ＧＤＰ成長率の推移
民間最終消費支出 民間企業設備 その他民需 公需 外需
（資料）内閣府経済社会総合研究所「四半期別ＧＤＰ速報」
( )内は年率換算値

１. 2026年 7-9月期の実質 GDPは、前期比▲0.0％（年率▲0.1％）と 4四半期ぶりのマイナ
ス成長となった（当研究所予測 10月 30日：前期比▲0.1％、年率▲0.3％）。 
２. 物価上昇率の高まりによる実質購買力の低下や天候不順の影響で民間消費が前期比
▲0.3％の減少となったことがマイナス成長の主因である。一方、高水準の企業収益を背
景に設備投資は前期比 1.6％の高い伸びとなった。 
３. 外需寄与度は前期比▲0.6％（前期比年率▲2.4％）と 3四半期ぶりのマイナスとなった。
財貨・サービスの輸出が前期比▲0.2％と減少する一方、代替調達の進展に伴う原油輸入
量の急増を主因として財貨・サービスの輸入が同 2.5％の増加となった。 
４. 原油の代替調達に伴う輸入増加による成長率の押し下げ（前期比寄与度▲0.5％）は、国
家備蓄の放出がほぼなくなったことによる公的在庫変動のプラス寄与（同 0.5％）によっ
てほぼ相殺されている。両者を合わせた成長率への影響はほぼ中立となった。 
５. マイナス成長の主因となった民間消費の減少は、天候不順による一時的な影響が大きい
が、こうした影響が解消されても、物価上昇の加速が消費の回復を妨げる可能性が高い。
現時点では、2026年 10-12月期はプラス成長に復帰するものの、民間消費が伸び悩むこ
となどから、前期比年率 0％台半ばとなり、マイナス成長の後としては低めの伸びにと
どまると予想している。 
ニッセイ基礎研究所 2026-11-16

### Page 2

名目 GDPは前期比 0.4％（前期比年率 1.6％）と 10四半期連続で増加し、実質 GDPの伸びを上
回った。GDPデフレーターは前期比 0.4％（4-6月期：同 1.0％）、前年比 2.4％（4-6月期：同 2.6％）
となった。輸入デフレーターが前期比 3.3％となり、輸出デフレーターの伸び（同 1.3％）を上回っ
たことが GDPデフレーターを押し下げたが、国内需要デフレーターが前期比 1.0％（4-6月期：同
1.4％）の上昇となった。

### Page 3

＜需要項目別の動き＞ 
民間消費は、物価上昇率の高まりによる実質購買力の低下や天候不順の影響で、前期比▲0.3％と
2四半期連続で減少した。2026年 4-6月期が前期比 0.0％から同▲0.3％へ下方修正されたため、民
間消費の減少は 2四半期連続となった。 
3月末の自動車税の環境性能割廃止に伴う自動車販売の大幅増加や、2027年 4月の省エネ基準引
き上げを見越した駆け込み需要によるエアコン販売の好調により、4-6月期に大幅に増加した耐久
財が、その反動で大きく落ち込んだことも消費の下押し要因となった。 
実質家計消費の内訳を形態別にみると、外食、旅行、宿泊などのサービスは前期比 0.1％と小幅
な増加となったが、自動車、エアコンなどの耐久財が前期比▲3.5％の減少となったほか、値上げが
続く食料品などの非耐久財が同▲0.2％、被服・履物、家具などの半耐久財が同▲1.4％の減少とな
った。 
名目雇用者報酬は前年比 5.5％と、4-6月期の同 5.3％から伸びが高まった。実質雇用者報酬は前
年比 2.5％（4-6月期：同 2.6％）と 10四半期連続で増加した。

住宅投資は前期比▲1.1％と 2四半期連続で減少した。新設住宅着工戸数（季節調整済・年率換算
値）は、建築物省エネ法・建築基準法改正前の駆け込み需要の反動で、2025年 1-3月期の 86.7万
戸から 4-6月期に 62.0万戸へ急減した後、7-9月が 71.9万戸と持ち直し、その後は概ね 70万戸台
半ばで推移している。GDP統計の住宅投資は住宅価格上昇の影響もあり弱めの動きとなっている。

設備投資は前期比 1.6％と 3四半期ぶりに増加した。設備投資が高い伸びとなった一因は、4-6月
期に特許権の海外譲渡に伴う研究開発投資の減少によって落ち込んだ反動である。ただ、その影響
を除いても、高水準の企業収益を背景に、人手不足に対応した省力化投資、デジタル化に向けた情
報関連投資、AI・半導体関連などの成長分野への投資、Eコマースの拡大に伴う建設投資などを中
心に、回復基調が続いている。

公的需要は前期比 1.8％と 2四半期ぶりに増加した。高校授業料無償化や公立小学校の給食費無
償化に伴う家計消費支出の減少分が計上され、4-6月期に前期比 1.5％の高い伸びとなった政府消費
がその反動で同▲0.0％と減少し、公的固定資本形成は前期比▲1.1％と 2四半期連続で減少した。
一方、原油輸入量の急減を受けた国家備蓄原油の放出により前期比・寄与度▲0.5％の大幅マイナス
となった公的在庫変動が、備蓄の放出がほぼなくなったことで、プラス寄与（同 0.5％）に転じた
ため、公的需要全体では前期比 1.8％の増加となった。

外需寄与度は前期比▲0.6％（前期比年率▲2.4％）と 3四半期ぶりのマイナスとなった。財貨・
サービスの輸出は前期比▲0.2％と 4四半期ぶりに減少した。半導体製造装置などの資本財輸出が好
調だったことから、財輸出は同 2.4％と増加したが、4-6月期に大幅に増加した研究開発サービスの

### Page 4

（2026年度後半は低空飛行が続く見通し） 
2026年 7-9月期のマイナス成長の主因となった民間消費の減少は、天候不順による一時的な影響
が大きい。ただし、こうした影響が解消されても、物価上昇の加速が消費の回復を妨げる可能性が
高い。消費者物価上昇率は 2026年 9月に 9ヵ月ぶりの 2％台となったが、原材料価格の上昇を耐久
財や日用品などの販売価格に転嫁する動きが今後さらに広がることから、2026年度末にかけて 3％
台まで高まる公算が大きい。物価上昇率の高まりによる実質購買力の低下を背景に 2026年度後半
の民間消費は弱めの動きが続くことが予想される。 
現時点では、2026年 10-12月期はプラス成長に復帰するものの、民間消費が伸び悩むことなどか
ら、前期比年率 0％台半ばとなり、マイナス成長の後としては低めの伸びにとどまると予想してい
る。続く 2027年 1-3月期は、物価上昇率が 3％台まで高まることに加え、飲食料品の消費税率引き
下げを控えた買い控えも影響し、民間消費が減少する可能性が高く、実質 GDP成長率は前期比年
率 0％台前半にとどまるだろう。2026年度後半の日本経済は物価高による民間消費の低迷を主因と
して、低空飛行が続くことが予想される。

本資料記載のデータは各種の情報源から入手・加工したものであり、その正確性と完全性を保証するものではありません。 
また、本資料は情報提供が目的であり、記載の意見や予測は、いかなる契約の締結や解約を勧誘するものではありません。

CENTRAL BANK MEETINGS

Bank of Thailand (BOT) (October 28)

We expect the BOT to maintain its policy 
rate at 1.00% at its 28 October meeting, 
extending its pause for a fourth 
consecutive decision as it balances 
inflationary pressures against uneven 
and subdued economic growth. Our view 
is aligned with fixed market income 
pricing. Headline inflation rose anew to 
2.8% yoy in September 2026, driven by 
higher energy, food, and core price 
increases, but remained within the upper 
end of the central bank’s 1-3% target 
range. As the BOT is still likely to look 
through what it views as temporary, 
supply-driven inflationary pressures, it 
will likely refrain from tightening policy, 
and keep interest rates accommodative 
to support the soft and uneven growth 
cycle.

European Central Bank (October 29)

We expect the ECB to extend its pause, 
keeping the deposit facility rate at 2.5%. 
Market participants will instead focus on 
the central bank’s assessment of rising 
French and Italian bond yields and 
widening spreads versus Bunds. While 
we do not expect direct references to 
individual countries, the ECB is likely to 
signal that recent developments in 
France reflect the country’s weaker 
fiscal and debt profile rather than a 
broader systemic concern. Policymakers 
will seek to differentiate between a 
localised bond sell-off and a Eurozone-
wide market disruption, justifying their 
decision to monitor developments rather 
than implement specific measures at this 
juncture. Meanwhile, inflation continues 
to rise, with little relief expected in 
upcoming readings given elevated 
global fuel prices. 
Bank of Japan (October 30)

The Bank of Japan is expected to keep 
the overnight call rate unchanged at 1.25% 
at this meeting, pausing after the 25bp 
hike in September. The BOJ is likely to 
assess the impact of the previous rate 
hike before taking further action. The 
recent stabilisation of the yen also 
reduces the urgency of another hike at 
this meeting.

The tightening bias should remain intact. 
Growth and inflation remain broadly on 
track, leaving room for the BOJ to revise 
up its FY2026–FY2027 GDP and CPI 
forecasts. Wage growth has also 
remained strong, with Rengo reportedly 
targeting wage increases of 6% in 2027, 
reinforcing the BOJ’s confidence that 
underlying inflation can be sustained 
around its 2% target. We continue to 
expect the policy rate to rise to 1.50% in 
December 2026 or January 2027, 
followed by a further increase to 1.75% 
by 2Q 2027.

FORTHCOMING DATA 
RELEASES

Hong Kong SAR

Export growth is expected to accelerate 
from 53.0% yoy in August to 55.0% in 
September, in line with the pickup in 
China’s export growth from 25.0% to 
36.3%. As a key re-export hub for China,

### Page 3

The Week Ahead October 23, 2026 
                                                                                                                                         3

Hong Kong should benefit from robust 
AI-related electronics trade, with 
electrical machinery, equipment and 
parts exports to Hong Kong accounting 
for 20.6% of China’s total exports. 
Import growth is also expected to remain 
firm, supported by resilient export 
orders and gradually improving 
investment sentiment, sustaining 
demand for intermediate and capital 
goods.

Singapore

Singapore’s industrial production (IP) 
likely remained in expansion for a 13th 
consecutive month in September 2026, 
although we expect growth to moderate 
to 7.6% yoy from August’s strong 15.4% 
yoy pace. IP growth continued to be 
underpinned by the electronics and 
precision engineering clusters, which 
has been key drivers of Singapore’s 
manufacturing strength, reflecting 
sustained demand for artificial 
intelligence-related hardware. However, 
performance across clusters remained 
uneven, with chemicals and biomedical 
manufacturing likely contracting, and 
tempering overall gains.

South Korea

The preliminary estimate for 3Q26 GDP 
is likely to show growth remaining solid 
at 3.2% yoy, slightly below 3.7% in 2Q 
and 3.8% in 1Q. Nominal GDP growth is 
likely to remain in double digits, 
significantly outpacing real GDP growth, 
thanks to improved terms of trade amid 
rising memory export prices. By 
expenditure, exports picked up in 3Q in 
both nominal and real terms, supported 
by stronger semiconductor shipments. 
Investment also appeared robust, as 
reflected in the equipment investment 
estimation index and machinery order 
index. Private consumption, however, 
appeared to weaken amid the sharp 
KOSPI correction since July following 
the introduction of leveraged ETF 
regulations. Our full-year GDP growth 
forecasts of 3.5% for 2026 and 2.4% for 
2027 remain on track.

Taiwan

The preliminary estimate for 3Q GDP is 
likely to show growth remaining strong 
at 12–13% yoy, close to 12.9% in 2Q, 
albeit below the peak of 15.4% in 1Q. 
Exports maintained strong double-digit 
growth in 3Q (44.6% yoy in nominal 
terms and 21.1% in real terms), supported 
by robust shipments of semiconductors, 
servers and other AI-related products. 
Machinery investment also sustained 
strong double-digit growth, underpinned 
by higher capacity utilisation in the ICT 
sector and a resulting increase in capital 
goods imports. Private consumption 
appeared to have strengthened further, 
supported by stock market gains, 
improving wage growth, and the 
associated wealth and income effects. 
Construction investment remained the 
weak spot, weighed down by elevated 
inventories in the new-home market and 
the ongoing need for inventory digestion. 
We maintain our 2026 and 2027 GDP 
growth forecasts at 11.6% and 5.6%, 
respectively, with risks tilted to the 
upside.

## Document 571: 凱基投顧_3533 嘉澤_向子慧_20261113

Source PDF: all report pdf/凱基投顧_3533 嘉澤_向子慧_20261113.pdf
Report source: line報告備份
Date: 2026-11-13
Pages: 9
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

十一月 13, 2026 研究網站: https://investment.kgisia.com.tw/Portal/Report/Index/Zh/R  Powered by the EFA Platform 1

動態更新 連接器 ‧ 台灣

嘉澤 
(3533.TW/3533 TT)

利潤率回溫與強勁伺服器營收將推升 2027 年 EPS 成長 
重要訊息 
嘉澤公布 3Q26 EPS 17.06 元，大致符合我們預期。受惠於 AMD (美) 與 
Intel (美) 推出新一代 CPU 平台，預期其伺服器營收將大幅成長，並成為推
升 2027 年 EPS 成長的主要動能。 
評論及分析 
3Q26 EPS 符合預期，利潤率已落底。3Q26 EPS 為 17.06 元 (季減 3.5%、年
減 17.6%)，符合凱基預期。毛利率 44.8% 及營業利益率 22.9% 亦符合預
期，利潤率季減歸因於原物料成本上升及伺服器營收占比由 2Q26 的 55.4% 
降至 53%，加上 PC 需求疲軟導致 CPU socket 產能利用率偏低。凱基預期 
3Q26 毛利率及營業利益率將落底。1Q-3Q26 EPS 為 56.11 元，年增 18%，整
體營收年增 18%，其中伺服器相關營收則年增 63%。 
4Q26 起利潤率逐步回溫，伺服器將持續為 2027-28 年營收成長動能。嘉澤指
引 4Q26 營收將季增低個位數百分比，其中伺服器相關營收成長較強，因 
AMD Venice CPU 放量 (儘管滲透率低於 5%)，且新產品營收成長 (例如快
接頭 (QD)、SOCAMM 及 Cable)。公司預期 4Q26 桌機相關營收將因 CPU 
升級而穩定，但筆電相關營收仍將疲弱。在產品組合改善下，毛利率及營業
利益率可望溫和季增。凱基微調 4Q26 EPS 預估至 18.18 元，2026 年 EPS 
預估為 74.28 元，年增 6%。至於 2027 年，嘉澤預期伺服器相關營收成長
將持續強勁，儘管通用型伺服器相關出貨受零組件長短料限制。不過，公司
指引 2027 年伺服器營收成長將優於 2026 年的年增 55-60%，因新 CPU 滲
透率提升，其針腳數較前一代增加逾 20%，ASP 亦高出逾 20%。公司預期 
AMD Venice CPU 滲 透 率 將 由  4Q26 的 低 於  5% 升至 2027 年 平 均 約 
20%，Intel Oak Stream CPU 則可能自 1Q27 起放量，2H27 滲透率最高達 
10-15%。這些 CPU 升級將帶動 PCIe Gen6 滲透率提升，並伴隨 ASP 上
升。加上 QD、SOCAMM 及 cable 的營收貢獻由約 5% 升至 2027 年的逾 
10%，凱基預期明年伺服器相關營收將成長 60%，占總營收 65%，高於 
2026 年的 54%。亦預期伺服器相關營收擴張將使毛利率自 2026 年的低檔
提升。嘉澤亦預期 2027 年工業與車用營收將持續成長雙位數，強於凱基預
估。據此，我們調整財務模型，將 2027 年 EPS 微調至 115.77 元 (年增 
56%)。 而我們預期 NPO socket ，以及對 Microsoft ( 美 ) 的  ASIC CPU 
(Cobalt) socket 出貨將於 2H27 開始貢獻營收，於 2028 年貢獻更多，至於 
Nvidia (美) 的 GPU socket 出貨，預計將為 2028 年獲利成長的另一動能。 
投資建議 
我們將目標價微幅上調至 2,370 元，相當 2027 年每股盈餘的 20 倍。預期
伺服器營收強勁且 AI 新產品營收成長，維持「增加持股」評等。 
投資風險 
原料價格上漲、新 CPU 平台導入落後及新台幣升值。

焦點內容  
1. 3Q26 EPS 17.06 元，符合我們預期，
且毛利率與營利率已觸底回升。 
2. 2027 年伺服器需求仍將維持強勁，但
零組件供應將是主要限制因素。受惠於
新一代 CPU 平台推出，以及 
SOCAMM、快接頭 (QD) 與 cable 高的
額 外 營 收 貢 獻 ， 將 推 升 伺 服 器 業 務 於
2026-27 年維持 50-60%的高速成長。 
3. 2028 年 NPO 與 Maia CPU 插槽營收貢
獻將於進一步放大。2027 年伺服器營
收比重將攀升至 65%，並於 2028 年持
續提高，將推升獲利率與 EPS 成長。
